Document 019: 2.3万亿美元“灰飞烟灭”！美国芯片股上一次跌这么惨，还是2001年，英伟达已经“跌掉一个亚马逊”

Source PDF: all report pdf/2.3万亿美元“灰飞烟灭”！美国芯片股上一次跌这么惨，还是2001年，英伟达已经“跌掉一个亚马逊”.pdf
Report source: WallstreetCN (Global)
Date: 2026-11-21
Pages: 6
Status: ok

### Page 1

本周，费城半导体指数跌逾 10% ，芯⽚板块市值蒸发约 2.3 万亿美元，创 2001 年以来最惨
单周表现。英伟达股价连续七周下跌，⾃ 10 ⽉⾼点以来市值缩⽔约 2.7 万亿美元。英伟达收
紧融资⽀持、 Anthropic IPO 抽⾛流动性，美联储鹰派⽴场再添压⼒， AI 交易遭遇集中抛
售，市场对增⻓前景的担忧升温。
摘要：
AI 交易的三⼤⽀柱同时松动，美国科技股遭遇猛烈抛售，市场对 AI 泡沫和经济增⻓放缓的担忧迅
速升温。
本周，费城半导体指数（ SOX ）重挫逾 10% ，创 2001 年以来最惨烈的单周表现，芯⽚板块整体
市值蒸发约 2.3 万亿美元。英伟达股价连续第七周下跌，创 2008 年以来最⻓连跌纪录，⾃历史⾼
点以来市值缩⽔约 2.7 万亿美元，相当于跌掉⼀个亚⻢逊。
抛售潮迅速蔓延⾄整个美股市场。标普 500 指数连续七个交易⽇下跌，单周跌幅超过 9% ，创近
年来最⼤单周跌幅；纳斯达克 100 指数单周下跌逾 10% ，年内涨幅悉数回吐。 VIX ⼀度突破 40 ，

市场恐慌情绪急剧升温。
值得注意的是，英伟达财报表现亮眼、 Anthropic 创纪录 IPO 相继登场，却都未能提振市场信
⼼。 AI 巨头融资态度转变、 IPO 抽⾛市场流动性，加上美联储鹰派⽴场不变，令市场开始重新审
视这轮 AI ⾏情的⽀撑基础。
⾸⻚ 资讯 快讯 ⾏情 ⽇历 APP VIP 会员 ⼤师课 ⻅闻历 搜索

### Page 2

芯⽚股遭遇 2001 年以来最惨单周抛售，英伟达跌掉⼀个亚⻢逊
本周，费城半导体指数下跌逾 10% ，芯⽚板块市值蒸发约 2.3 万亿美元，创 2001 年以来最惨烈的
单周表现。
英伟达同样未能幸免。尽管公司最新季度业绩和业绩指引均超出市场预期，股价仍连续七周下
跌，创 2008 年以来最⻓连跌纪录。周五股价进⼀步⼤跌，收于 2025 年 5 ⽉以来最低⽔平。
从 10 ⽉历史⾼点以来，英伟达股价累计下跌约 47% ，市值缩⽔约 2.7 万亿美元，相当于⼀个亚⻢
逊。⽬前公司市值约 3.03 万亿美元，已低于 Alphabet 。
值得注意的是，英伟达基本⾯并未明显转弱：公司连续第 17 个季度超越卖⽅预期，第四季度营
收指引达到 1300 亿美元，⾼于市场预期的 1250 亿美元，还将 2028 财年增⻓预期从 70% 上调⾄
75% 。市场担忧的焦点，正在转向 AI ⽣态系统的融资和增⻓模式。
英伟达收缩 “AI 央⾏ ” ⻆⾊，⽣态系统融资⽀持转向审慎
据 MarketWatch 报道，英伟达新任董事⻓ Harvey Jones 明确表⽰，⻩仁勋不会回归公司，同时

释放出收紧客⼾融资⽀持的信号：只有在项⽬具备⼟地、电⼒和建筑等实体条件后，公司才会
提供担保⽀持。
⾼盛 TMT 团队将此解读为英伟达正在主动收缩 “AI 央⾏ ” ⻆⾊。此前，英伟达不仅是芯⽚供应商，
也通过融资担保等⽅式⽀持 AI ⽣态系统。如今态度转向审慎，可能冲击依赖英伟达担保或承购⽀
持的纯新云计算公司。

### Page 3

Citadel Securities 的 Nohshad Shah 指出，像⻩仁勋这样的创始⼈能够推动公司承担职业经理⼈
难以向董事会交代的⾼⻛险投资。⾼盛 Rich Privorotsky 则认为，英伟达此前被视为 AI ⽣态系
统的 “ 多头看跌期权 ” ，如今这⼀⻆⾊已经逆转，甚⾄成为市场的 “ 空头看跌期权 ” 。
Anthropic 创纪录 IPO 反成流动性⿊洞， AI 资产遭遇集中抛售
Anthropic 原本被视为 AI 产业信⼼的⼜⼀次验证。这家 Claude 开发商以⾼于发⾏区间的价格定
价，融资规模达 1073 亿美元（含向芯⽚和云供应商的 260 亿美元配售），完全稀释估值约 1.5 万亿
美元。
但 IPO 未能提振市场，反⽽引发流动性冲击。 Anthropic 股价以 111.50 美元开盘，随后迅速遭到抛
售。纳斯达克 100 指数从盘前上涨 2% 转为下跌 2% ， AI 股票篮⼦也从上涨 4% 转为下跌 2% ，标普
500 指数当⽇市值蒸发约 1.3 万亿美元。

市场⼈⼠认为，投资者为参与 IPO 提前筹集资⾦，加剧了其他资产的抛售。⻩⾦、加密货币、铜
和英伟达股票均在 IPO 前数⽇遭到卖出。彭博援引分析指出，⽐特币在发⾏前的异常下跌，与投
资者筹集认购资⾦的⾏为相符。
⾄周五收盘， Anthropic 股价跌⾄ 83.10 美元附近，⼏乎回到 83 美元的发⾏价，较周四 115.10 美元
的⾼点下跌逾 25% 。承销商已全额⾏使超额配售权，额外出售 1.47 亿股，意味着稳定股价的潜
在⼯具进⼀步减少。

### Page 4

美联储鹰派施压，市场脆弱结构放⼤跌势
美联储本周进⼀步强化紧缩信号。理事 Waller 重申⽀持加息 50 个基点的⽴场，指出约 70% 的核⼼
服务项⽬涨幅仍超过 3% ，美联储可能需要收回去年全部三次预防性降息，甚⾄采取更多⾏动。
他还表⽰，可能需要通过政策⾏动 “ 震慑市场 ” 。
10 ⽉会议纪要也显⽰，部分官员曾倾向于加息，许多与会者认为 AI 需求持续对供给形成压⼒并
推⾼价格。彭博数据显⽰，美联储官员鹰鸽表态指标已升⾄ 2023 年以来最鹰派⽔平。
市场结构则进⼀步放⼤了抛售。据⾼盛 Prime 数据，基本⾯多空净杠杆本⽉再下降 7.6 个百分
点，降⾄五年多来最低⽔平。做市商在周五期权到期前持有⼤量空头伽⻢头⼨，使下跌更容易
触发进⼀步对冲卖盘。
⾼盛数据显⽰，周三标普 500 期货盘⼝深度平均仅为 420 万美元，是过去四年来流动性最差的交
易⽇之⼀。不过，资⾦并未全⾯撤离：截⾄ 11 ⽉ 18 ⽇当周，全球股票基⾦净流⼊ 592 亿美元，科
技基⾦净流⼊ 123 亿美元，显⽰部分投资者仍在逢低买⼊。

债市转向增⻓担忧， AI 交易⾯临重新定价
与股市同步下跌的还有美债收益率。尽管美联储官员持续释放鹰派信号， 10 年期美债收益率仍
连续五个交易⽇下⾏，从 5.17% 降⾄ 4.90% ，结束了⾃ 2007 年以来最⻓的持续⾼于 5% 的时期。
⾼盛 Rich Privorotsky 认为，⻓端收益率可能已经开始反映政策失误的⻛险。收益率下⾏的同
时，盈亏平衡通胀率也在收缩，这⼀组合表明市场关注点正从通胀转向经济增⻓和流动性。
换⾔之，市场担忧正在发⽣变化：投资者不再只关注通胀顽固和⾼利率，也开始担⼼紧缩政策
可能过度，以及 AI 投资热潮能否持续⽀撑经济增⻓。
Baird 董事总经理 Ted Mortonson 表⽰， 2026 年上半年的市场充斥着过度亢奋、投机和⾃满，⽽
本⽉以来市场情绪已经转向恐惧与恐慌。
Privorotsky 的判断则更为谨慎：在央⾏政策真正转向之前，市场反弹仍可能是卖出机会。

### Page 5

⻛险提⽰及免责条款
市场有⻛险，投资需谨慎。本⽂不构成个⼈投资建议，也未考虑到个别⽤⼾特殊的投资⽬标、财务状况或需要。⽤⼾应考虑本⽂
中的任何意⻅、观点或结论是否符合其特定状况。据此投资，责任⾃负。

Document 046: 20261116【投研報告】市場快訊_輝達產品時程恐因領導層變動遞延，台股供應鏈重挫

不另行通知。
輝達產品時程恐因領導層變動遞延，台股供應鏈重挫
2026 年 11 月 16 日
一、市場動態
1. 輝達執行長閃辭衝擊延燒，台股重挫逾 2500 點：黃仁勳於 11 月 12 日辭去輝達總裁、
執行長及董事職務，由財務長 Colette Kress 暫代執行長，資深董事 Harvey Jones 出
任首任董事長，市場擔憂產品時程與決策速度出現不確定，加上本周財報即將發布，
加深市場觀望情緒。台股今日受上週五美股半導體重挫拖累開低走低，收最低至
41,040 點、下跌 2635 點或 6.03%。台積電跌 135 元或 5.74%，聯發科跌逾 7%，台

達電、創意及台光電、欣興等 PCB／載板族群觸及跌停，高價 AI 股成為賣壓重心。
2. AI 晶片族群同步降溫，資金由「換股」轉為「降低整體曝險」：輝達換帥後，市場原預
期資金將轉進 AMD、博通及 ASIC 等替代方案，惟上週五美國半導體族群全面回檔，
替代受惠股跌幅甚至更大，顯示投資人擔憂的已非單一公司接班問題，而是 AI 投資循
環的整體風險。
二、下跌原因
1. 領導層更替落地，產品時程與決策速度出現不確定：輝達在此事件之前，約 60 名高階
主管直接向黃仁勳匯報，權力高度集中，接班空窗期決策速度恐下降。即使在其任內，
年更節奏已觸及供應鏈物理極限，Rubin CPX 已移出路線圖，Kyber 機櫃傳延至 2028
年。新任管理層若偏重成本與執行穩定，路線圖可能改為「大世代兩年、中間推出強
化版」，800V HVDC、共同封裝光學（CPO）、 M9 等級銅箔基板等升級導入時點恐再
遞延。
2. 規格升級放緩衝擊「本夢比」，高價零組件股首當其衝：台廠 2027 年成長有相當部分
來自 Vera Rubin 單櫃價值約為 GB300 兩倍，而單櫃價值主要來自規格升級。市場擔

憂高階液冷、HVDC 電源與光互連若由 2027 年遞延至 2028 年放量，供應鏈單價、
毛利率與 EPS 成長預估恐面臨下修；缺乏技術突破支撐的高本益比族群評價修正，今
日 PCB／CCL、散熱及電源族群跌停家數最多，即反映此一疑慮。
3. 生態系融資與出口管制成為新增變數：市場估計輝達每季自由現金流約 500 億至 600
億美元，若過渡期暫緩投資及融資交易，生態系資金供給可能暫時減少 500 億美元以
上；據報 Kress 曾反對為 OpenAI 提供最高 1,050 億美元擔保，新增融資審核恐趨嚴，
依賴輝達融資的 NeoCloud 及其供應商受影響較大。此外，事件涉及晶片流向中國之
指控，美方據報擬將訂定較嚴格出口限制，市場估潛在曝險約占輝達營收一成。
4. 期中選舉後，可能的出口管制收緊以及短線獲利了結需求：此事件涉及晶片流向中國之
指控，出口管制收緊將衝擊整體 AI 晶片；台股上週反彈近 4,000 點，短線獲利了結需
求升高，避險心態未退，放大 AI 權值股賣壓。

圖一：輝達執行長更替後，可能影響路徑分析
資料來源：元大銀行整理，Bloomberg，UBS
三、市場看法
1. 2027 年出貨大致鎖定，衝擊先落在評價而非營收：Vera Rubin 已於 8 月量產出貨、
2027 年放量；輝達對供應商之採購與產能承諾達 2,790 億美元，台積電、HBM 及主
要組裝廠 2027 年產能多已預訂。廣達法說表示迄今未見客戶調整訂單，鴻海亦預期
明年 AI 機櫃出貨高雙位數成長。依 ToAlpha 基本情境推算，輝達 CoWoS 份額相對
原本路徑 2027、2028 年僅分別減少 2 與 4 個百分點，CoWoS 總量僅減約 0.6%與

0.8%，影響主要在 2028 年後的產品節奏。
表一：輝達份額變動對 AI 平台與台股供應鏈之影響（情境推估）
項目（相對原本路徑） 2027 年 2028 年
輝達占 CoWoS 需求份額 −2 個百分點 −4 個百分點
輝達平台出貨量 −4.4% −8.9%
AMD 與 ASIC 平台出貨量 +2.5% +5.8%
CoWoS 總量（台積電、封測） −0.6% −0.8%
以輝達平台為主之台股 AI 營收 −3.4% −6.7%
GPU 與 ASIC 兩邊都有之台股 AI 營收 −1.3% −2.3%
以 ASIC 為主之台股 AI 營收 +1.5% +3.6%
資料來源：Bloomberg，元大銀行整理；此為 Bloomberg 情境推估之 AI 營收影響，非盈餘預估
2. 台股供應鏈由分化轉為齊跌，ASIC 族群抗跌性消失：依曝險分組，10 月 29 日至 11
月 12 日以輝達平台為主者平均下跌 14.0%，GPU 與 ASIC 兩邊都有者下跌 17.0%，以
ASIC 為主者下跌 9.4%，同期加權指數下跌 7.1%。兩邊都有之族群多為前波漲幅大的

規格升級零組件股，跌幅反而最深，顯示市場賣的是「評價」而非「訂單」；今日台股
盤勢延續上周五費半情緒，以 ASIC 為主及兩邊都有之代表股平均重挫逾 8%，以輝達
平台為主者跌約 4.4%，顯示賣壓轉向前波漲多、評價較高的 ASIC 與規格升級股，市
場已由「區分受益者」轉為「全面降低 AI 曝險」，短線錯殺機會浮現。

### Page 3

確性不做任何擔保，如有錯漏或疏忽，本行及本行之任何董事或受僱人，毋須負任何法律責任。任何人因信賴此等資料而做出或改變投資決

3. 算力多元化早於本次事件，Google TPU 與 AMD 最直接受惠：根據彭博在本次事件發
生前的統計，市場預估輝達占全球 CoWoS 需求比重將由 2026 年 56%降至 2027 年
45%，AMD 由 9%升至 20%，聯發科由 3%升至 7%，預計本次事件發生後，此趨勢
可能進一步加劇。由於 AMD、博通與雲端業者自研 ASIC 同樣高度依賴台積電先進製
程及台廠組裝與零組件，訂單多在台灣供應鏈內部移轉，台灣在 AMD、Google 供應
鏈同樣扮演重要角色。

4. 歷史經驗顯示 CEO 更替後股價無一致方向，基本面才是中期關鍵：回顧美股大型科技
公司交棒案例，計畫性交棒如 Apple、Microsoft 後 12 個月股價分別上漲 77.1%與
20.1%，但同為規劃性交棒的 Amazon 卻下跌 35.3%；突發撤換的 Intel 兩案，3 個月
內均下跌逾 9%，12 個月後則轉為正報酬。惟過去歷史經驗僅能作為參考，輝達後續
走勢仍取決於事件發展與基本面變化而定。

5. 本週三大事件為短線關鍵驗證點：（一）輝達將於美東 11 月 17 日（台灣時間 11 月 18
日凌晨）公布第三季財報，為首度由暫代執行長主持之法說會，焦點在 2028 會計年度
約七成成長指引是否重申、Vera Rubin 放量節奏及新增融資態度；（二）Anthropic 預
計 11 月 18 日定價上市，為檢驗 AI 市場熱度之指標；（三）黃仁勳預計感恩節前赴國
會作證，牽動晶片出口管制法案走向。若財報與指引穩健，市場有望由情緒面回歸基本

面。
四、投資建議
1. 科技股短期審慎、中期仍正向，AI 供應鏈基本面持續具支撐：黃仁勳辭任使輝達面臨
管理層接班、企業治理及政策調查等不確定性，加上 Fed 升息預期及 AI 資料中心監
管疑慮，市場短線波動可能延續。然而，該事件並未直接改變 AI 算力需求，且上週
CoreWeave Q3 財報營收年增 168%、台積電 10 月營收年增 59.7%，均顯示 AI 基礎
建設投資仍具動能。整體對科技股維持短期審慎、但中長期基本面仍在，台灣及日本
等具技術競爭優勢、獲利與現金流穩健的半導體及 AI 供應鏈亦值得留意。惟市場焦點
已由資本支出規模轉向投資報酬與現金流，後續仍須關注輝達財報及管理團隊能否維
持客戶信心。
2. 以股債平衡穿越波動，善用多元資產降低單一事件風險：台股權重高度集中於台積電
與 AI 供應鏈，對單一事件敏感度高。建議將集中度過高的台股科技部位，適度轉向全
球型或多重資產商品，搭配中天期美國公債及高信評投資等級債穩定收益；待接班人
選、財報指引與出口管制方向明朗後，再逐步提高風險資產比重。

市場焦點
美國
• 10月CPI月增0.4%，較前月放緩，但核心CPI連續三個月維持月增0.3%，顯示整體通膨雖已脫
離高峰，服務類價格壓力仍具黏著性。能源與住房成本持續支撐通膨，反映需求與供給失衡
問題尚未完全改善，也使市場下修利率預期的空間受到限制。整體而言，本次數據傳遞「通
膨下降但速度偏慢」訊號，聯準會維持審慎立場的必要性仍高，市場將持續關注後續核心通
膨與勞動市場變化。
輝達
• 輝達共同創辦人黃仁勳於11月12日正式辭任執行長及董事職務，由財務長柯蕾特・克雷斯暫
代執行長，公司董事會則啟動接班人遴選。此次人事變動不僅涉及企業治理，更可能影響全
球AI產業的競爭格局。
• 輝達既有的研發團隊、CUDA開發者基礎及產品路線圖仍具延續性，短期營運不至於因人事更
迭立即出現結構性改變，後續關鍵在於接班團隊能否延續原有技術與生態系統優勢。

美債
• 2027年新國會就任後，兩黨將圍繞企業稅、富人稅以及部分即將到期的個人稅收減免展開拉
鋸。即便民主黨推動對企業與高收入者加稅，也極可能遭到白宮行使否決權，導致開源受阻
而節流難以達成。短期內，市場因預期大規模單邊財政刺激受阻，美債殖利率可能出現階段
性回落；但中長期看，6%以上的赤字率意味著美債供給壓力依然沉重，長端美債（10年期、
30年期）仍面臨較高的期限溢價。

美國CPI月增放緩，核心物價僵固限制政策轉向空間
• 能源與住房價格推升整體通膨：10月CPI月增0.4%，雖較9月的0.6%放緩，但仍高於聯準會達成物價穩定所需水準，其
中能源價格月增0.7%、汽油月增1.0%，住房成本月增0.3%，兩者合計成為10月通膨主要推手，顯示地緣風險與住宅供
給不足仍持續壓抑通膨降溫速度。
• 核心通膨僵固反映服務需求仍具韌性：扣除食品與能源後的核心CPI月增0.3%，已連續三個月維持相同增幅；醫療服務、
汽車保險、教育及休閒服務價格持續上升，反映勞動市場與服務需求仍具支撐力；即使二手車價格月減1.2%，仍不足以
抵銷服務類價格壓力，顯示通膨回落進程可能較市場預期緩慢。
• 市場看法：本次數據傳達「通膨降溫但速度不足」訊號，市場可能提高聯準會維持高利率時間更長的預期，雖短期有利

美元與短天期美債殖利率維持高檔，但科技成長股評價將受利率壓力牽制。投組配置建議受惠高利率環境與能源價格支
撐的短天期美國公債、美元資產、高品質金融股及能源類股，並降低長天期債券曝險，以因應利率維持高檔風險。

輝達領導權更迭，短期營運不至於因人事更迭立即出現結構性改變
• 輝達領導權更迭，考驗AI生態系統的延續性：輝達共同創辦人黃仁勳於11月12日正式辭任執行長及董事職務，由財務長柯
蕾特・克雷斯暫代執行長，公司董事會則啟動接班人遴選。此次人事變動不僅涉及企業治理，更可能影響全球AI產業的競爭
格局。輝達的領先優勢並非僅來自GPU晶片性能，更建立在黃仁勳主導的整合策略與扁平化管理（約60名高階主管直接向黃
仁勳匯報），包括CUDA軟體平台、高速網路、AI伺服器及完整運算系統，形成難以複製的產業生態系。
• 短期營運不至於因人事更迭立即出現結構性改變：新任領導團隊無法維持跨部門整合效率，可能影響產品迭代速度與供應鏈
協調，為AMD等競爭對手創造追趕空間。不過，輝達既有的研發團隊、CUDA開發者基礎及產品路線圖仍具延續性，短期營

運不至於因人事更迭立即出現結構性改變，後續關鍵在於接班團隊能否延續原有技術與生態系統優勢。
事件時間軸與事件發生後主要主要機構評等與目標價

輝達領導層變局影響：短期業績有撐，長期考驗技術決策速度
• 投資人最關心的公司盈利能力，輝達在未來一至兩年內仍具備極高的能見度，輝達的霸主地位並非僅建立在個人魅力之上，
而是龐大工程師團隊、難以撼動的CUDA軟體生態系統，以及緊密的全球半導體供應鏈。
• 短期財務預測不變：由於既有的客戶訂單、供應鏈產能分配與產品路線圖均已確立，預期這起人事變動不會改變輝達在
2027至2028年的核心獲利預測。
• 長期繼任風險浮現：黃仁勳過去能在晶片架構、軟體、網路與系統之間做出極快速的權衡決策，並深度維繫核心客戶與供應
商關係，失去這位靈魂人物後，繼任成本不會立刻反映在財報上，但可能會在未來數個產品週期中逐步顯現，考驗新管理層
在面對超微(AMD)等競爭對手時的應變與交易推進速度。
• 華為受限於製造瓶頸，短期難撼動輝達主導地位：在硬體算力供給方面，中國本土替代方案仍面臨嚴峻技術天花板。自

2020年美國切斷華為與台積電的代工合作後，華為被迫轉向中芯國際，不僅研發週期拉長，效能提升也相對有限。預估
2026年輝達的H100等效晶片產量高達2,300萬顆，而華為昇騰(Ascend)系列產量僅約90萬顆，不足輝達的 4%。
輝達主導全球AI晶片算力 華為2026年AI晶片產量預計不足輝達的4%
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.11.13

美國對中國管制恐全面收緊
• 地緣政治與政策轉向：相較於公司內部營運交接，黃仁勳離任對美國出口管制政策的衝擊來得更為直接且劇烈。過去黃仁勳
是華府內部反對收緊對華晶片管制最具影響力的游說者，甚至能直接與總統川普溝通並促成政策放寬(如先前撤銷H20限制
與放行H200銷往中國)。隨著輝達在華盛頓的政治影響力減弱，美國政府與國會正加速封堵中國獲取先進AI算力的四大途徑
(本土生產、合法進口、轉運走私、海外遠程訪問)。即將於深圳舉行的APEC峰會前夕，美國強硬派立法動能增強，恐使明
年1月到期的中美貿易休戰協議更難延續，中國取得美國先進算力的管道可能在輝達內部繼任風險發酵前就已全面收緊。
• 小結與投資建議：黃仁勳的離任對全球AI產業而言是一場雙面刃衝擊，短期內，輝達憑藉深厚的技術護城河與訂單積壓，基

本面依然穩健；但中長期來看，公司將面臨領導層過渡的陣痛，同時因失去強力的華府游說管道，中國市場的營收貢獻與算
力供應鏈將面臨更嚴格的合規審查與政策封鎖，投資建議增持非中國區雲端服務商(CSP)與美國AI基礎設施標的，降低高度
依賴中國AI算力轉運或灰市貿易的周邊供應鏈。
中國獲取先進AI算力的四條途徑美國在前沿模型領域保持微弱領先
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.11.13

中國出口續強，高科技需求推升貿易順差突破兆美元
• 中國出口續強，AI與高科技需求推升貿易順差突破兆美元：10月進出口數據優於預期，出口年增率35.2%較9月略為放緩，但
仍維持雙位數成長，進口年增41.6%，受惠於全球AI熱潮與科技投資擴張，積體電路出口金額年增率高達137.1%，不僅遠高
於整體出口增幅，也反映中國在半導體與電子供應鏈中的重要地位持續提升，此外近期美中雙方同意將部分關稅豁免措施再
延長兩個月，市場普遍解讀為雙邊貿易關係短期將保持和諧，此舉有助於降低企業接單與出貨的不確定性，維持電子、機械
設備、汽車零組件等與美國市場連結較深的產業的短期外銷動能。
• 展望後市：在AI需求帶動、高科技產品出口強勁，以及美中關稅延長帶來的政策利多支撐下，中國外貿仍展現韌性，不過，

若全球經濟成長放緩或地緣政治風險再度升溫，出口成長動能仍可能面臨挑戰，後續需持續觀察海外需求與貿易政策變化。
10月中國進出口數據優於預期

中國PPI加速上漲掩蓋了能源和科技領域外的通縮壓力
• 中國PPI加速上漲掩蓋了能源和科技領域外的通縮壓力：中國10月通膨數據顯示，經濟價格結構出現明顯分化，PPI年增率由
9月的5.0%升至5.7%，優於市場預期，且已連續第三個月走升，反映能源價格上漲及AI硬體成本增加推動上游價格回升，但
CPI年增率卻由0.9%降至0.7%，低於市場預估，從細項來看，10月價格回升主要集中於能源與科技硬體，然而食品價格跌幅
擴大，抵銷能源帶來的漲價效果，核心CPI由1.0%降至0.8%，反映一般消費需求與服務消費動能仍不足。
• 投資建議：市場預期中國GDP平減指數有機會自2022年以來首度轉正，但若缺乏更積極的財政刺激或消費支持政策，經濟多
數領域仍將面臨通縮壓力，後續需持續關注國際原物料價格走勢，及中國內需復甦力道對通膨的影響，後市來看AI供應鏈及

科技硬體族群仍具相對受惠機會。
資料來源:bloomberg,2026.11.10
通膨支撐主要來自能源和科技產業 2026年的中國為供給驅動型再通膨
資料來源:bloomberg,2026.11.10

國安基金激勵台股上週大漲，市場暫時看淡輝達換帥消息
• 國安基金第10次進場：11/6財政部宣布進場，為台股投資人注入信心，激勵台股上週週漲逾10%。國安基金表示，美國期
中選舉後主要股市重挫，加上外資延續10月以來賣超趨勢，短期內市場信心明顯不足，啟動成立以來第10次國安基金進場。
• 護盤持股聚焦少數核心權值股：國安基金早期操作傾向分散買入20檔左右，近幾次護盤則集中台積電為首的權值股。前次
護盤(第九次)台積電資金占比高達六成，其他權值股包含鴻海、日月光、聯發科、台達電、廣達、富邦金及台塑。
• 觀點：(1)從歷史績效統計來看，國安基金進場後短期大多能激勵台股上漲，但中期績效表現分歧且波動大，顯示總體經濟
趨勢才是關鍵。(2)AI運算晶片教父黃仁勳因婚姻醜聞宣布閃辭，市場初步認為需求基本面未變，台股不跌反漲。(3)整體來

說台股企業因此黃仁勳事件的衝擊不大，短線國安基金支撐股市信心，後市仍需聚焦在總體經濟環境及企業財報。在AI投資
反覆遭到市場質疑的環境下，建議持股聚焦具全球供應鏈龍頭地位的企業，適度減碼單純由消息面支撐的個股。
資料來源：財政部、Bloomberg，兆豐銀行整理。2026.11.13。

美債發行指引微調，期限結構調整彈性提升
• 刪除關鍵措辭，釋放長債供給調整訊號：美國財政部最新季度再融資聲明表示，未來數季預計維持名目公債與浮動利率
債券（FRN）標售規模不變，但將過去慣用的「至少維持目前規模」修改為「預計維持目前規模」，反映決策彈性提高。
市場解讀此舉代表財政部已開始為未來調整長天期公債供給預留政策空間，降低長債供給僵固性的訊號明顯。
• 兼顧融資成本與需求結構成為政策核心：財政部強調將持續評估不同期限債券的供需變化、聯準會縮表進程及發債成本，
並以「長期最低融資成本」為主要原則。在財政赤字仍大、短券需求強勁背景下，未來若長天期需求改善不足，不排除
透過調整期限結構降低利息負擔與市場吸收壓力。
• 後市觀察：若2027年後長債供給增速放緩，將有助緩解期限溢酬上升壓力，但強勁就業及通膨風險仍可能推升殖利率，

對20年以上美債及投資等級債券評價形成支撐。投資上可關注2至7年期美國公債及投資級債，兼顧票息與利率風險；
長債則留意美國20年以上公債、長存續期間投資級債，以等待供給政策與利率方向更明朗。
資料來源：Bloomberg， 2026.11.10。Note: Expectations based on survey of primary
dealers designated by the Federal Reserve Bank of New York..

民主黨期中選舉橫掃國會，但削減赤字的空間有限
• 民主黨期中選舉橫掃國會，但削減赤字的空間有限：從歷史經驗與2026年當前的財政結構來看，執政黨失去兩院主導權後，
美國財政赤字率（赤字占GDP比重）呈現短期擴張受限，但中長期難以下降的格局。截至2026財年（9月30日止），美國聯
邦赤字已達近 2兆美元（占GDP約 6.1%–6.2%），公眾持有債務突破 32兆美元（總債務破 40兆美元）。由於人口老化帶來
的社會安全（Social Security）與醫療保險（Medicare）屬於強制性支出，加上每年超過 1兆美元的債務利息負擔，兩黨在
不觸碰核心福利改革的情況下，削減赤字的空間有限。
• 美債供給壓力依然沉重：2027年新國會就任後，兩黨將圍繞企業稅、富人稅以及部分即將到期的個人稅收減免展開拉鋸。

即便民主黨推動對企業與高收入者加稅，也極可能遭到白宮行使否決權，導致開源受阻而節流難以達成。短期內，市場因預
期大規模單邊財政刺激受阻，美債殖利率可能出現階段性回落；但中長期看，6%以上的赤字率意味著美債供給壓力依然沉
重，長端美債（10年期、30年期）仍面臨較高的期限溢價。
資料來源：Silber bulletin，2026.11
各機構預估2027年財政赤字率
資料來源：各機構，2026.11

顧 • AI資本支出短期壓抑現金流，亞洲供應鏈獲利動能持續擴散：美國超大型資料中心營運商未來幾年仍將持續擴大資本支出，
短期恐壓抑自由現金流，並引發市場對大型科技企業財務狀況的疑慮，導致美股科技股及亞洲科技、半導體類股短線波動。
然而，隨著全球AI資料中心建置持續推進，2027至2028年AI供應鏈需求預料仍將維持強勁，訂單與獲利動能亦持續向亞洲
擴散。摩根預估，亞洲科技企業自由現金流將由目前約2,450億美元增至2028年的6,330億美元；野村預估，台灣、日本及
韓國資訊科技指數2027年預估每股盈餘（EPS）成長率更分別達37%、41%及42%。
• 企業現金流改善挹注股東回饋，投資仍須留意評價與波動風險：充裕的自由現金流亦有助企業提升股東回饋，南韓與日本企
業本財政年度迄今回購金額分別達約140兆韓元及12.7兆日圓，進一步為股市提供支撐。儘管台灣、日本及韓國股市已累積

可觀漲幅，且美國聯準會（Fed）升息可能帶來評價壓力，但科技股中長期表現仍取決於企業獲利成長及預估上修動能。

2. 本資料提及之經濟走勢預測不必然代表基金之績效，基金投資風險請詳閱基金公開說明書。
3. 基金經理公司以往之經理績效不保證基金之最低投資收益；基金經理公司除盡善良管理人之注意義務外，不負責本基金之盈虧，亦不保證最低之收益 ，投資人申購
前應詳閱基金公開說明書。
4. 本資料所載內容如涉及商品資訊，商品相關資訊仍以經核可之條款及文宣(DM)為主，本行網站已備有公開說明書可供查閱基金應負擔之費用及相關投資風險。投
資人申購基金係持有基金受益憑證，而非本資料提及之投資資產或標的。
5. 本資料所載內容取自本公司相信可靠之資料來源，且為特定日期之判斷，本公司不保證其即時性、精確性及完整性，亦不提供或嘗試遊說客戶做為投資之判斷依據。

資料內容邇後若有變更，本公司將不做預告或主動更新。投資人於決策時應審慎衡量本身風險，如因信賴本資料內容而做出或改變投資決策，須自行承擔結果，本
公司及本公司所屬員工，均不負任何損害賠償及其他法律上之責任。
6. 本資料內容之著作權屬兆豐銀行所有，禁止就所載資料任何形式之抄襲、引用或轉載。
7. 本資料所載部分基金涉及非投資等級債，投資人投資以非投資等級債券為訴求之基金不宜占其投資組合過高之比重。基金經金管會核准，惟不表示絕無風險。由於
非投資等級債券之信用評等未達投資等級或未經信用評等，且對利率變動的敏感度甚高，故本基金可能會因利率上升、市場流動性下降，或債券發行機構違約不支
付本金、利息或破產而蒙受虧損。部分基金不適合無法承擔相關風險之投資人。基金經理公司以往之經理績效不保證基金之最低投資收益；基金經理公司除盡善良
管理人之注意義務外不負責本基金之盈虧，亦不保證最低之收益，投資人申購前應詳閱基金公開說明書。
8. 基金年化配息率計算公式：每單位配息金額÷除息日前一日之淨值×一年配息次數×100%。「年化配息率」為估算值。當月配息率計算公式：每單位配息金額÷除

息日前一日之淨值×100%。
9. 基金配息率不代表基金報酬率，且過去配息率不代表未來配息率；基金淨值可能因市場因素而上下波動。投資人因不同時間進場，將有不同之投資績效，過去之績
效亦不代表未來績效之保證。本資料如以基金過去績效進行模擬投資組合之報酬率時，僅為歷史資料模擬投資組合之結果，不代表本投資組合之實際報酬率及未來
績效保證，不同時間進行模擬操作，其結果亦可能不同。
10. 部分基金配息前未先扣除應負擔之相關費用。基金的配息可能由基金的收益或本金中支付。任何涉及由本金支出的部份，可能導致原始投資金額減損。各基金由本
金支付配息之相關資料，投資人可至各投信基金公司及總代理人網站查詢。

漢唐(2404 TT) Buy

3Q26 獲利優於預期，客戶建廠需求暢旺，給 Buy 的投資建
議。 
投資建議： 3Q26 獲利優於預期，加上在手訂單金額約 1,939 億
元，有助於未來漢唐營運，給 Buy 的投資建議。 
漢唐 3Q26 獲利優於預期：漢唐 3Q26 營收 275.06 億元，
QoQ+5.47%；稅後純益 45.69 億元，QoQ+21.43%；稅 後 EPS 23.97
元。3Q26 稅後純益優於原先預估的 39.27 億元，主因毛利率優
於預期。 
漢唐目前在手訂單金額約 1,939 億元：漢唐 01~05/2026 年接單
合約金額約 674 億元，截至 05/2026 年已簽約未認列之金額(在
手訂單)約 1,939 億元。在手訂單約 24~36 個月可以認列大部分。
目前在手訂單金額的施工地區，有 2/3 在台灣，1/3 在海外。 
24~36 個月認列大部分的在手訂單可能是保守估計，2H26 在手
訂單金額可能還會再增加。 
漢唐對營運樂觀：漢唐表示目前建廠需求遠大於供給，因目前
人力短缺，尤其是在台灣，暫時沒辦法去接其他大型客戶的訂
單。由於 AI 與相關需求，現在客戶建廠的需求會延續到 2029
年，漢唐對整體產業的展望表示樂觀。

### Page 2

個股報告 
                                        2026 年 11 月 13 日

2 群益投顧 
  ESG：  
永續環境是漢唐經營理念之綠色承諾，因應全球環保節能的需求，我們透過
工程設計、系統整合提升工程效率，減少工程過程中能耗。漢唐集成響應政
府綠色採購辦法，我們從原物料取得、工程施作、運輸配送、完工至客戶使
用階段，優先要求廠商提供低污染、可回收、再利用之環保產品，以達到廢
棄物減量之目標。 
漢唐集成根著台灣，從初創之施工安裝小公司成長為專業工程集成公司，有
賴於各地政通人和，因此，漢唐集成本著感恩的心回饋在地，不斷捐助弱勢
及支持培育英才，期望肩擔起企業之「社會責任」，讓台灣變得更美好。漢
唐股東會由全體股東組成，對公司重大事項進行決策，定期聽取董事會報
告，為公司最高決策機關；董事會為最高治理機關，董事會成員皆恪盡善良
管理人之注意義務，擘劃公司的經營政策與檢視財務績效，並確保公司營運
遵守各種法令；為完善公司治理運作、強化公司競爭力，董事會設置審計委
員會與薪資報酬委員會來健全董事會運作；董事會之下亦設有獨立之稽核
室，定期執行稽核業務並向審計委員會與董事會呈報稽核結果。 
公司簡介： 
漢唐集成成立於 1982 年，為一以「全面性整合服務」方式，專業提供高科
技產業建廠之系統集成服務公司；主要業務範圍包括高科技廠房各系統，包
括電腦、控制、無塵室、機電、電信系統之施作與整合服務。 
漢唐目前在手訂單金額約 1,939 億元： 
漢唐 01~05/2026 年接單合約金額約 674 億元，截至 05/2026 年已簽約未認
列之金額(在手訂單)約 1,939 億元。在手訂單約 24~36 個月可以認列大部
分。目前在手訂單金額的施工地區，有 2/3 在台灣，1/3 在海外。 
24~36 個月認列大部分的在手訂單可能是保守估計，2H26 在手訂單金額可
能還會再增加。 
漢唐未來還有先進封裝廠的訂單： 
大客戶於美國建廠的第三期工程的訂單目前漢唐已經有接到一部份，還沒
有認列到手訂單金額中，客戶的先進封裝廠的訂單還沒發包。第二期工程進
度希望在 2026 年認列約 85%，第三期工程的認列狀況要看客戶，未來還有
先進封裝廠的訂單。 
美國營收已超過台灣： 
漢唐沒有透露前兩大客戶的營收占比。2025 年營收占比_地區別：台灣 65%、
美國 25%、其他 9%。 1Q26 台灣 40%、美國 56%、其他 4%。美系記憶體廠
在台灣蓋無塵室，漢唐還是主要的供應商。 
漢唐對營運樂觀： 
漢唐表示目前建廠需求遠大於供給，因目前人力短缺，尤其是在台灣，暫時
沒辦法去接其他大型客戶的訂單。由於 AI 與相關需求，現在客戶建廠的需
求會延續到 2029 年，漢唐對整體產業的展望表示樂觀。

### Page 3

個股報告 
                                        2026 年 11 月 13 日

3 群益投顧 
其他補充： 
漢唐的費用比較固定，管理費用包含員工酬勞。漢唐追求與客戶長期合作，
如果是原物料上漲導致成本增加，客戶可以接受漲價。 
目前漢唐在全球的員工人數約 1,700 人，美國、台灣都會再增加人力，之後
也是要看下包廠商，看是否能擴大供應鏈。

6 群益投顧 
  【投資評等說明】 
  評等 定義 
  強力買進(Strong Buy)  首次評等潛在上漲空間≥ 35%  
  買進(Buy)  15% ≤首次評等潛在上漲空間<35%  
  區間操作(Trading Buy) 5%≤首次評等潛在上漲空間<15%

中立(Neutral)  
無法由基本面給予投資評等 
預期近期股價將處於盤整 
建議降低持股

【投資評等調整機制】 
  投資評等係「首次」給予特定個股投資評等時，其前一日收盤價相較 3 個月
目標價之潛在上漲空間計算而得。個股投資評等分為四個等級，定義如上。
爾後的投資評等係依循「首次評等」，直到停止推薦。  
「強力買進」、「買進」及「區間操作」均有 upside 目標價。差別僅在於，
首次評等時潛在報酬率不同。「中立」則無目標價。 
一旦我們給予特定個股「強力買進」、「買進」或「區間操作」之投資評等，
就是責任的開始，爾後將透過各式研究報告作定期性、持續性追蹤基本面及
股價變化，直到停止推薦。 
停止推薦情境： 
1. 達目標價。 
2. 雖未達原訂目標價，但檢視基本面、訊息面、籌碼面等多方訊息，研
判股價上漲空間已然有限，將適時出具降評報告。 
3. 推薦後股價不漲反跌。

【免責聲明】 
  本研究報告僅提供予特定人之客戶作為參考資料「非經同意不得轉載」。我
們並不確保此資訊的完整性與正確性，投資人應了解，報告中有關未來預測
之陳述可能不會實現，因而不應被依賴。而且此報告並非根據特定投資目的
或依預定對象之財務狀況所撰寫出來的，因此，此研究報告的目的，既非對
投資人於買賣證券、選擇權、期貨或其他證券相關之衍生性商品提供詢價服
務，亦非作為進行交易的要約。投資人應注意到相關證券之價值及收益，可
能會有無預警地上升或下降，產生投資回報金額可能比原始投資來得少的
情形。

Update
King Yuan Electronics Co Ltd  | Asia Pacific
Asia Pacific Summit 2026
Feedback
Continuing capacity build with unchanged 2027 demand: The company mentioned
that AI demand remains strong and customers (especially the largest customer) are
still asking for more clean room build. 2027 demand is almost confirmed with no
changes over the past month.
Capacity allocation: US capacity will fully support the largest customer initially and
the capacity in Taiwan is fungible between different customers. For example, given

4Q26 weakness for consumer/smartphones, the company has shifted spare capacity
to meet other demand, including the testers from Advantest and Teradyne.
Margin should still be in the upward trend: The priority for the company now is to
have customers consign the equipment if they are asking for more capacity, lowering
the deprecation pressure. Also, given the tight capacity, the company would serve
the products with higher margin (generally AI demand and longer test time product)

Valuation Methodology and Risks
King Yuan Electronics Co Ltd (2449.TW)
Base case, residual income model. Key assumptions: cost of equity of 9.8%, intermediate-term

Risks to Upside
n Rise in shipments for core customers such as MediaTek
n Strong AI demand from both GPU and ASIC
n Penetration of wafer sorting business
n Rise in IDM outsourcing to OSAT

Risks to Downside
n Overall non-AI demand remaining weak
n Significant testing market share loss

Analyst Stock Ratings
Overweight (O). The stock's total return is expected to exceed the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next
12-18 months.
Equal-weight (E). The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over

the next 12-18 months.
Not-Rated (NR). Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage
universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.
Underweight (U). The stock's total return is expected to be below the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next

12-18 months.
Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months.
Analyst Industry Views
Attractive (A): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be attractive vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated
below.
In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.
Benchmarks for each region are as follows: North America - S&P 500; Latin America - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI Latin America Index; Europe - MSCI Europe; Japan - TOPIX; Asia -
relevant MSCI country index or MSCI sub-regional index or MSCI AC Asia Pacific ex Japan Index.

Source PDF: all report pdf/[000660] SK하이닉스(000660) 여전히 강력하다SK하이닉스(000660) 여전히 강력하다여전히 강력하다 - 기업.pdf

### Page 1

00

SK 하이닉스 (000660/KS) 
여전히 강력하다 
3Q26 영업이익 83 조원으로 예상 상회 
SK하이닉스의 3Q26 영업이익은 83조원 (+38% QoQ)으로 예상을 상회했다. 메모
리 공급 부족 심화, HBM4 반영 시작에 따른 메모리 가격 상승이 환율 하락을 상쇄했
기 때문이다. 출하 DRAM +10%, NAND -1%, 가격 DRAM +28%, NAND 
+24%, 영업이익률 DRAM 82%, NAND 72%, HBM 판가는 HBM4 출하가 시작되
며 37% 수준 상승한 것으로 추정된다. 업황은 여전히 예상을 상회하고 있다.

4Q26 분기 영업이익 100 조원 시대 진입 전망 
4Q26 영업이익은 105조원 (+27% QoQ)으로 분기 100조원 시대에 진입할 것으로 
전망한다. 메모리 가격 강세가 지속되는 가운데, 1c 출하 가속화, M15X 램프업 본격화 
등으로 출하량 상승이 기존 예상을 상회할 것이다. 또한 HBM4 의 Cross-over 시작 
및 수율 안정화로 HBM 가격, 수익성 제고 동반이 지속될 것이다. 출하는 DRAM 
+24%, NAND +4%, 가격은 DRAM +8%, NAND +7%를 예상한다.

2027 년 영업이익 488 조원 (+70% YoY)으로 상향조정 
SK 하이닉스의 2027년 영업이익을 488 조원 (+70% YoY)으로 12% 상향 조정한
다. 원/달러 환율 전망치를 기존 1,340 원에서 1,315 원으로 하향 조정했음에도, 공급 
부족 심화 속 2027년향 HBM 가격 인상 및 예상을 상회하는 수율, 1Q27 진행될 모바
일 DRAM 가격 인상 등을 반영했기 때문이다. LTA 가격 역시 개별 약정된 범위 내 
시장 상황을 반영할 수 있는 구조라는 점을 감안하면, 지속 상승할 것이라 전망한다.

높은 주주환원 의지 확인, 높아진 실적 가시성, 지나친 저평가 
투자의견 매수, 목표주가 4,000,000원을 유지한다. SK하이닉스는 LTA에 대해 기
존 5년에서 추가 연장에 대한 논의가 진행 중이며, 2029년까지 HBM, DRAM은 사실
상 완판, HBM 수요는 공급 가능 물량의 2 배를 상회, 2030 년까지 메모리 수요 우위 
국면 전망을 밝혔다. AI 인프라 투자의 강력한 명분과 메모리 수요의 구조성, 잠재적으
로 매우 높은 LTA 위반 비용을 감안하면 실적 가시성과 안정성 제고가 동반되는 국면
이다. 100 조원 수준의 주주환원은 2026 년 예상 FCF 의 66% 수준, 분기별 자사주 
매입, 소각 지속 계획을 통해 높은 주주환원 의지를 보여주고 있다. 실적 Duration 장
기화, 주주환원을 감안하면 12MF P/E 3.4X 수준의 현 주가는 현저한 저평가 국면이
다. 과도한 우려의 되돌림과 메모리 밸류에 대한 재논의 국면 진입을 전망한다.

당사는 자료공표일 현재 해당기업과 관련하여 특별한 이해 관계가 없습니다. 
종목별 투자의견은 다음과 같습니다.

Document 317: EcoNews of 9 November 2026 (2026-11-09)

Source PDF: all report pdf/EcoNews of 9 November 2026 (2026-11-09).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-11-09
Pages: 2

### Page 1

The bank
for a changing
world
6
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.41 / 9 November 2026ECONEWS
Find out more in our scenario and forecasts
ADVANCED ECONOMIES
In brief. The week was dominated by large market swings triggered by
the US midterms results. In the end, energy prices end the week much
lower, bond yields somewhat lower and stock prices down in tech-heavy
markets and slightly up elsewhere, notably in Europe. Incoming data
confirmed a picture of resilient growth overall. The Bank of England

hiked its policy rate by 25 bps and signaled it could do more. Market
pressure on France eased somewhat.
EUROZONE
Energy pushes inflation up, but underlying pressures remain rela -
tively contained. Headline inflation rose to 4.0% y/y in October, its
highest in three years, strengthening the case for another ECB rate
increase in December. Energy remained the main driver, while core in-
flation rose by 2.6%. The ECB wage tracker points to negotiated wage

growth slowing to 2.3% by September 2027. Meanwhile, retail sales fell
0.7% m/m in September and the average interest rate on new business
loans increased 13bp to 3.90%.
To watch next week: September trade balance, Q3 GDP second esti -
mate and employment (Friday).
France
EVs boost activity, market pressure eases somewhat. After already
bolstering household consumption in Q3, new car registrations rose
7% y/y in October (+4% y/y YTD), driven by another surge in electric

car sales (+81% y/y in October; +78% y/y YTD). In parallel, manufac -
turing output rebounded by 0.5% m/m in September, supported by
the automotive sector (+7.5% m/m but -5.5% y/y) and capital goods
(+2.6% m/m and +4.8% y/y). However, industrial production fell by
0.5% m/m (and 1.3% y/y), as a result of a decline in electricity produc -
tion (-4.2% m/m). The composite October PMI was revised down by
1.8 points from the flash estimate to 46.6 (-4.5 m/m), dragged down by

services. This sharp downgrade likely reflects the hit to sentiment from
the October 17 riots; however, precedents suggest activity itself should
not be visibly impacted. Appropriately indifferent to the meandering
journey of the draft budget through Parliament, the OAT-bund spread
narrowed to 155 bps (-10 bps in one week) amid a broad-based decline
in government bond yields across advanced economies; Thursday’s
long-term debt auction, the last of the year, was well-subscribed. Stri-

ving to meet its deficit target of 5.4% of GDP in 2026, the government
announced EUR3.8bn of spending cuts to offset EUR3.7bn in additional
expenditure. The High Council for Public Finance nevertheless sees the
deficit reaching 5.5%. The 2027 budget will start being discussed by
the Senate from next week, in its original version, after the national
Assembly unanimously rejected its amended version that would have
led to a 5.9% deficit.
To watch next week: Q3 unemployment rate and the Banque de France

monthly business survey (Tuesday).
Germany
Industrial activity picks up. New orders rebounded 4.4% m/m in Sep -
tember and industrial production increased 0.7%, with the auto sec -
tor delivering particularly striking gains of 10.5% in orders and 17.1%
in production. Mechanical-engineering orders also recovered (+8.3%),
while pharmaceutical production increased by 8.0%. By contrast, large
orders in aerospace, shipbuilding, rail and defence fell by 10.4%, while

machinery and equipment production declined by 6.5%. October's com-
posite PMI was revised up to 53.4, signaling continued strong expan -
sion.
Italy
Services keep the economy above water as manufacturing slips back.
The composite PMI eased to 50.8 in October, its lowest since May.
Manufacturing returned to contraction at 49.4, its weakest reading
since January, while services strengthened to 52.0 and recorded a fifth
consecutive month of growth. Both sectors reported increasing input-

cost and output-price inflation.
UNITED KINGDOM
The BoE hikes and leaves the door open to more. The Bank of England
raised Bank Rate by 25bp to 4%, its first increase since August 2023, in a
more hawkish than expected 8-1 vote, with Catherine Mann favouring
a 50bp hike. The November Monetary Policy Report sees CPI inflation
peaking around 5% y/y by mid-2027 and upgraded Q4 2026 growth to
2.2%, while cutting the 2027 and 2028 growth projections. The com -

posite PMI meanwhile improved to 52.9, its highest since February,
with new business growing at its strongest pace in nine months and
private-sector employment increasing for the first time in 25 months.
To watch next week: Q3 GDP, business investment, industrial produc -
tion (Thursday).
UNITED STATES
Post election market jitters and a hot jobs report. The Democrats’
unexpectedly sweeping victory in the midterms (the largest wave
since 1974) initially led markets to rally on (i) relief that the President

recognised the results, and (ii) hopes of a swift end to the Iran war
(oil prices and bond yields down, stock prices up). But this was soon
followed by a sharp equity sell-off on 5 November, with the S&P 500
down 4.4% and the Nasdaq down 5.0%, erasing USD2.7 Trillion of mar-
ket capitalization in one day. Why? Because of the realization that vo -
ters strongly supported AI-restrictive measures on the ballot (46 out
of 47) and candidates — for Congress and Governor seats — in favour

of curtailing data centers buildout. By the week-end, however, senti -
ment had turned around again, helped in part by a much stronger than
expected jobs report: payrolls increased by 165,000 in October against
a 70,000 consensus, while the previous two months were revised hi -
gher and unemployment edged down to 4.1%. Average hourly earnings
rose 0.4% m/m, their strongest monthly pace since November 2025.
Manufacturing activity also strengthened, with the ISM rising to 55.5,

and the non-manufacturing index easing to a still buoyant 54.5.
To watch next week: October CPI (Tuesday); October PPI (Friday).
JAPAN
Wage growth accelerates. Scheduled contractual earnings increased
3.7% y/y in September and total wage growth accelerated to 4.6%,
helped by strong bonus payments. Real wage growth remained posi -
tive at 2.2% y/y.
EMERGING ECONOMIES
Portfolio outflows. According to IIF data, non-resident portfolio invest-
ment flows recorded USD33.8bn of outflows in October, the largest

monthly outflow since March and up from USD19.3bn in September.
Equities accounted for USD31.7bn, led by USD28.3bn from emerging
Asia, as doubts over AI-investment returns and profit-taking hit North
Asian markets particularly hard. Taiwan and South Korea suffered a
further sharp market correction in the first week of November.

### Page 2

7
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.41 / 9 November 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
Find out more in our scenario and forecasts
ASIA
PMI: expanding except for the Philippines. The ASEAN PMI rose to 53.1
in October from 52.1, pointing to faster expansion. India remained ro -
bust despite a slight decline (54.7). In Taiwan and South Korea, the
manufacturing PMIs declined but remained expansionary, at 54.9 and
51.3, respectively. Indonesia’s real GDP growth accelerated to 5.5% y/y

in Q3 2026 from 5.3% in Q2 marking its fastest pace since 2013.
China
The PBOC steps in as equity correction deepens. The central bank in -
creased its use of liquidity tools designed to support equity purchases,
including the special relending facility for share buybacks and stake in-
creases and a third round of the Securities, Funds and Insurance Com-
panies Swap Facility, for which 31 institutions applied for more than
RMB100bn. The measures follow heavy stock-market losses between

June and October. The PBOC also raised the reserve-requirement ratio
on foreign-currency deposits to 6% from 4%, effective 15 November, in
an effort to slow the renminbi's appreciation.
Weak domestic inflation pressure: In October, CPI inflation slowed to
0.7% y/y from 0.9% in September. Fuel prices increased by 20.2% y/y,
food prices fell -2.9% and core inflation slowed to 0.8% y/y from 1.0%.
Producer price inflation continued to rise (reaching 5.7% vs. 5.0% in

September), principally driven by higher energy and electronics prices.
To watch next week: External-trade data (Tuesday).
EMEA
Central Europe
PMIs expansionary except in Romania. Poland’s PMI hit the 50-mark
for the first time since April 2025 thanks to export orders. PMI was
also higher in Czech Rep. (55), supported by new orders, output and
employment. Hungary’s PMI fell marginally to 53.1.
Poland and Czech Republic central banks hold and hike respectively,

as expected.
Gulf
PMIs resilient except Qatar (deteriorating further to 46.3).
Saudi Arabia: Massive budget deficit in sight. Saudi Arabia recorded a
budget deficit of USD22.5bn in Q3, narrowing by 5% y/y. But over the
first nine months of 2026, budget deficit reached USD65.1bn, up from
USD48bn in 2025, despite a rather good revenue performance (+2.8%
total; +7.8% for non-oil revenues; -1.3% for oil revenues). The 8% rise in
spending in Q1-Q3 partly due to the war will make it difficult to meet

the 2026 revised target (deficit of 4.9% of GDP).
LATIN AMERICA
PMIs remain weak across the region, with only Colombia in expansion.
Brazil’s central bank pauses rate cuts, whereas Mexico’s central bank
cuts rates whilst signalling an upcoming pause. On 4 November, Bra -
zil’s central bank held its Selic rate at 13.75%, interrupting a series of
five consecutive cuts, amid rising inflation (4.6% y/y in September, up
from 4.2%), a sharp post-election real depreciation on fiscal concerns,

and higher energy prices. The central bank also revised up its 2027
inflation forecast to 4.7% (from 3.9%) and removed forward guidance
hinting at further cuts. Mexico’s central bank unexpectedly cut its po -
licy rate by 25bp to 6.25% on 5 November. This decision came despite
above-target inflation (3.5% against a 3% target), a peso depreciation
of more than 9% against USD since September, and relatively strong Q3
GDP growth (3.2% y/y). The central bank signaled that rates would now

remain on hold for an extended period.
COMMODITIES
Geopolitical relief delivers a dramatic energy-price reversal. Mar -
kets have priced in the reduced risk of short-term conflict escalation
through a sharp decline in prices. Beyond this, however, they lack di -
rection, wavering between positive signals (the resumption of indirect
negotiations between the belligerents) and less favorable ones (in -
creased vessel damage in the strait). Brent dropped 7.6% over the week

to USD96.8/bbl as markets priced a reduced risk of near-term conflict
escalation. European gas prices fell almost in lockstep, declining 7.7%
to EUR87.6/MWh. US commercial crude inventories continued to de -
cline, although more modestly than over the preceding four weeks.
Diesel crack spread’s reaction to US elections has been much more
limited than for crude. EU gas storage ended the refilling season at 75%
of capacity (vs. 80% goal).
y/y = year-on-year

m/m = month-on-month
bp = basis point
pp = percentage point
mb = million barrels
MBtu = Million British Thermal Units

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
TODAY’S INSIGHTS
Financial Markets
•  Share markets rallied across much of the globe. In the
US, reports of an upgraded outlook for CoreWeave, an
oversubscribed IPO for Anthropic and a stabilisation in
Nvidia supported renewed optimism around AI which

buoyed tech stocks, seeing the S&P 500 (+1.4%) and
Nasdaq (+1.7%) both rally, though the smaller gain for the
Dow Jones (+0.5%) shows the rally was more narrowly
based.
•  Across the pond, the Euro Stoxx 50 continued to build on
recent momentum with another solid gain (+1.0%), while
London’s FTSE 100 bounced back from a modest decline
yesterday (+0.8%). Stocks were up across most of Asia,
including Tokyo (+1.3%), Shanghai (+0.4%) and Seoul
(+2.6%), with only the Hang Seng (–1.0%) bucking the

wider trend. The ASX 200 rose 0.3% yesterday, but weaker
trading in futures overnight points to a mixed open this
morning.
•  Cash trading in bond markets were closed in the US for
Veterans Day, but trading in futures points to a continued
rise in yields following hotter-than-expected inflation
data received earlier in the week, which all but cemented
a December FOMC rate hike. Beyond December, markets
are also pricing in another two rate hikes over the course of

2027.
• Yields were little changed elsewhere, with the 10Y Bund
and Gilt steady on the day. The 10Y JGB meanwhile
continued to sell-off, with yields jumping 10bps to 2.80%
having effectively unwound the rally over late last week.
ACGBs were also weaker during local trading, seeing yields
rise 7bps on the 3Y and 6bps on the 10Y, following the
sharp rise in global yields the day prior post US CPI. Swaps
markets point to no hikes nor cuts from the RBA through to

end-2027 at present.
•  The USD received a solid bid overnight, buoyed by sharp
inflows into AI and tech stocks that improved risk sentiment.
The DXY finished 0.7% higher at around 102.9, the
strongest since April 2025. That move has pushed the Euro
down 0.6% back to a USD1.10 handle, the weakest since
May 2025, while the Yen slipped 0.7% to JPY159.9, the
weakest since the before the early-September rally. While
moves in the AUD was comparatively small overnight,

slipping 0.2% to around the USD0.6850 mark, that move
builds on recent downward momentum and has the currency
delicately positioned near 2026’s lows.
•  Oil prices rose slightly overnight, stabilising somewhat after
recent declines amid ongoing uncertainty around the future
of the US-Iran War, as President Trump downplays the
possibility of further strikes on Iran all while negotiations
show little progress. Brent rose 1.0% to US$92.4/bbl. The
rebound in risk sentiment buoyed metals, with LME copper

up 1.0% to US$13,817/mt while LME aluminium jumped
1.9% to US$3,068/mt. Gold prices meanwhile dipped 1.2%,
falling back below the US$4,000/oz mark, building on recent
momentum post US CPI which saw real yields jump higher
International Data
There was no major data out yesterday.
Domestic Data
In Australia, the value of dwelling finance approvals surprised
materially to the downside in Q3, the 15.5% decline marking
the largest quarterly decline on records going back to 1991.

The decline looks to be mostly volume-driven, with the number
of loans falling sharply compared to the average size of loans.
Across segments, investor lending values dropped by nearly a
third (see here).

Document 381: Productivity_ reasons for optimism (2026-11-21)

### Page 1

#gdp #australia #employment #housing #rba #productivity
Productivity: reasons for optimism
By Pat Bustamante
Senior Economist, Westpac
08:37 November 17 2026 Share
The supply-side outlook is improving as capital deepening strengthens, reallocation drags
fade and AI diffusion gathers pace.
The supply-side outlook is looking brighter. A pickup in capital deepening and a fading ‘reallocation’
drag are already evident in the Annual National Accounts, with AI diffusion offering further upside.

This gives us greater conviction that productivity growth can return towards 1% or more over time,

### Page 2

Growth in 2024-25 was a little firmer than first thought
The 2025–26 figures show somewhat firmer economic activity and business investment than previously
estimated, particularly in 2024–25. The economy grew by 2.3% in year-average terms, broadly in line
with the initial quarterly National Accounts estimate of 2.4%. However, growth in 2024–25 was revised
up by 0.3ppts to 1.6%, largely reflecting stronger private business investment. Business investment is

now estimated to have increased by 1.1% in 2024–25, compared with the initial estimate of a 0.2%
decline. The revisions do not materially alter the broader productivity story, but they do suggest that the
economy was growing a little more strongly for the same rate of inflation in 2025.
above the RBA’s assumption and closer to Treasury’s. The recovery illustrates why it is important to
focus on longer-term trends in productivity growth rather than on the latest year or two, which can be

volatile.
Capital deepening is returning. Investment is picking up and, outside mining, market-sector capital
per hour worked is now at a record high, rising a strong 1.5% in 2025–26.
The reallocation headwind is easing. The shift of labour towards non-market industries, which
generally have lower measured productivity levels, has reduced the level of aggregate productivity by
around 1ppt over the past 15 years. With the expansion of the non-market sector plateauing, this

drag looks set to fade.
Productivity growth is about more than capital. Multifactor productivity, which captures how efficiently
businesses combine labour and capital, was the dominant driver of market-sector productivity growth
in 2025–26. Meanwhile, stronger investment in IT, utilities and professional services should provide a
further lift as new capital is put to work.

### Page 3

The investment upswing is broadening
The Annuals also provide a richer picture of the supply side, including investment by industry and the
evolution of the capital stock, details that are not available in the quarterly accounts. These data point
to a notable lift in investment in the market (ex-mining) part of the economy, where investment has
gone from around 10% of nominal GDP in to just over 11.5%. Outside of this, investment has been
softer, particularly in the mining sector.

### Page 4

Within the market sector, investment by the IT industry, increasingly focused on data centres,
has risen to its highest share of nominal GDP since the technology boom of the 1990s. Utilities
investment, including renewables and associated infrastructure, is stronger still, at its highest share
since 1983, while investment by the professional services industry has reached a record high.

### Page 5

Across the non-market sector (including health, education and public administration), investment has
also held at a higher share of the economy, driven by the health and public administration (including
defence) industries.

### Page 6

Investment is translating into capital deepening
The investment upswing is also beginning to translate into capital deepening as industries look to
rebuild capital stocks after a period of soft growth. Outside mining, market-sector capital per hour
worked is at a record high and well above its pre-pandemic level. That is an encouraging
development, particularly given that the sector accounts for 70% of total employment.
There are important divergences beneath the aggregate. Mining continues to experience capital

shallowing, as investment has been insufficient to cover wear and tear and capital requirements
associated with additional workers. By contrast, the capital stock in the non-market sector appears to
have caught up with the surge in employment we have seen since the pandemic, with capital per
worker increasing for the first time since the pandemic.

### Page 7

A major productivity headwind is starting to fade
Capital deepening will help support productivity growth across the economy. The Annuals show that
productivity in the market sector (excluding mining) continues to grow by around 1% in year-average
terms, while mining remains a drag on aggregate productivity (down 0.8% in 2025–26 and almost 30%
since 2017).

### Page 8

At the same time, a major recent headwind is easing as the rapid expansion of the non-market sector
approaches slows. This matters for aggregate productivity. Productivity growth reflects both gains
within industries, through capital deepening, technology and better processes, and the ‘reallocation’ of
resources between industries with different productivity levels.
Measured productivity in the non-market sector is considerably lower than across much of the market

economy, partly reflecting measurement issues (output in these sectors is generally not well measured,
particularly when there are quality changes). As employment shifted towards these industries, this
compositional effect weighed on aggregate productivity. Our estimates suggest it has reduced the
level of aggregate productivity by a significant 1ppt over the past 15 years and almost 0.3ppts
over the three years to 2025–26.

### Page 9

Productivity gains were about more than capital
It wasn’t only capital deepening that drove productivity growth. Multifactor productivity (MFP), which
captures how efficiently businesses combine labour and capital through changes in processes,
technology, know-how, was the dominant driver of market-sector productivity growth this year. This
gives us more conviction that the supply side is normalising, and productivity growth will improve from
its recent lows.

### Page 10

Where productivity is improving, and where it isn’t
At the industry level, some of those recording stronger investment are also beginning to deliver
stronger productivity outcomes, although the picture remains uneven.
IT stands out at the positive end of the spectrum, alongside improving outcomes in manufacturing,
administrative and support services, and transport.. These are also industries where the diffusion of
AI and other digital technologies could deliver further productivity gains. Utilities and

professional services should also benefit as stronger investment translates into greater capital intensity
and efficiency.
At the other end, mining remains a major drag, consistent with the capital shallowing evident in the
investment and capital-stock data. Construction is another notable laggard, with productivity continuing
to deteriorate despite strong demand from housing, population growth, renewable-energy infrastructure
and data-centre projects. Construction productivity fell around 2% in 2025–26 and almost 21% over the

13 years from 2012–13 to 2025–26.

### Page 11

A more constructive supply-side story
The Annuals provide a more constructive supply-side picture than the aggregate productivity figures
alone suggest. Market-sector productivity outside mining continues to advance, the reallocation drag
from the expanding non-market sector looks likely to fade, and capital deepening is strengthening
across important parts of the economy. The strong MFP outcome in the market sector gives us more
conviction that the supply side is normalising, and productivity growth will improve going ahead.

The critical question is whether the strong investment underway in technology, professional services,
electricity and related infrastructure translates into stronger productivity growth as that new capital is
put to work. If it does, the improvement in capital intensity evident in the Annuals could mark the
beginning of a more durable lift in Australia’s productivity performance.

### Page 12

CENTRAL BANK MEETINGS

Bank of Korea (November 26)

We expect the BOK to raise its policy 
rate by 25bp to 3.25% at its November 
meeting, although the decision is likely 
to be close. The BOK's October minutes 
showed that most board members 
continued to favour further tightening. 
Recent economic indicators, including 
exports and PMIs, have remained solid, 
while CPI inflation stayed elevated at 2.8% 
yoy, above the BOK's 2% target.

That said, equity market volatility has 
increased amid concerns over AI 
financing risks, potential US AI 
regulatory constraints and intensifying 
US-China technology rivalry. These 
uncertainties could weigh on business 
confidence and domestic consumption, 
adding to the case for caution. 
Nevertheless, we think the BOK will 
place greater weight on domestic 
economic fundamentals than on short-
term market volatility, and continue to 
lean towards a 25bp hike.

FORTHCOMING DATA 
RELEASES

Singapore

We expect Singapore’s inflation to 
remain contained in October 2026, with 
headline and core inflation easing to 1.9% 
yoy and 1.7% yoy, respectively, from 2.2% 
yoy and 2.0% yoy in September, as 
favourable base effects kicked in. The 
moderation reflected smaller positive 
contributions from electricity & gas and 
retail & other goods, contained services 
inflation, and a slower increase in private 
transport costs as car price growth 
eased, despite a slight uptick in food 
inflation.

We expect industrial production to 
moderate to 1.0% yoy in October 2026, 
from 3.4% yoy in September, reflecting 
high base effects after output surged by 
15.0% yoy in the same period last year, 
the strongest monthly growth in 2025. 
Beneath the softer headline, robust 
artificial intelligence-related demand 
likely underpinned precision engineering 
and electronics production, although 
electronics growth may have moderated. 
Biomedical manufacturing likely also 
provided support, consistent with the 
sharp 253.1% yoy rise in pharmaceutical 
exports, despite unfavourable base 
effects.

We anticipate that 3Q26 final GDP 
growth will be revised higher to 5.6% yoy 
and 1.3% qoq sa, from advance estimates 
of 5.4% yoy and 1.1% qoq sa, mainly 
reflecting stronger-than-initially-
reported manufacturing expansion. 
Attention will shift to the 2027 outlook, 
where we expect the government to 
forecast growth of 2.5-4.5%.

Hong Kong SAR

Export growth is expected to remain 
robust at 61.8% yoy in October, 
supported by China’s strong export 
growth of 35.2% and continued demand 
for AI-related electronics. Broad-based 
strength across Asian markets should 
further support Hong Kong’s re-export 
trade. Import growth is also expected to

### Page 3

export orders and improving investment 
sentiment, which continue to support 
demand for intermediate and capital 
goods.

### Page 3

Helen Belopolsky, Miha Hribernik | This Month in Geopolitics: November 2026: Key events, themes and trends to watch in the month ahead |  November 2026
US-China talks, a possible Trump-Xi-Putin trilateral meeting, and any signs of a China-Japan diplomatic
thaw will be critical watchpoints. The priorities of the summit between representatives of 21 Asia-Pacific
economies are set to include efforts to boost innovation, digital connectivity, supply-chain resilience and

support for an open multilateral trading system. During the summit, presidents Trump and Xi are expected to
meet for the third time this year. This meeting will provide signals on the state of China-US talks, including the
operationalisation of new bilateral trade and investment boards, and on the likelihood of another extension of
the Busan trade truce beyond January 2027. President Trump has also indicated he is open to a trilateral US-
China-Russia meeting during the summit, possibly covering nuclear arms control and the Russia-Ukraine war.

Investors will also closely watch whether Japanese Prime Minister Takaichi and President Xi meet on the
sidelines, following reports that Tokyo is hoping to arrange a meeting. However, the bar for a formal
breakthrough will likely remain high. As we noted in a report earlier this year, a full normalisation of relations
and an easing of Chinese economic restrictions – which include rare-earth export controls – may require a
lengthy period of diplomatic re-engagement and backchannel diplomacy.

A divided government could mean less change at home but more abroad. Third-party polling and betting
markets forecast Democrat control of the House, and an increasing probability of flipping the Senate. 80% of
respondents in our monthly poll now say living costs will influence their vote – the most since April. For
markets, a divided government could mean less domestic legislative change, but not necessarily a more
predictable US foreign policy. Losing Congress could leave theadministration’s geopolitical and geoeconomic

toolkit largely intact.
In our view, weapons stockpiles, inflationary pressures and long-end yields may matter more than Congress
in constraining theadministration’s choices in 2027 and 2028. A Democratic House could dial up oversight of
the administration, launch investigations, force votes on War Powers Resolutions, and use appropriations bills
to press for restrictions on departmental funding. This may generate a steady stream of attention-grabbing

headlines, but whether it decisively affects the event-driven risk that matters for markets (e.g. blocking a tariff
or preventing a limited military strike) remains in doubt. If domestic policymaking becomes more challenging,
foreign policy could become a top priority for the Trump administration.
In the spotlight for November 2026: US midterms and APEC Summit
US midterm elections (3 November)
APEC Economic Leaders’ Meeting in Shenzhen (18-19 November)

### Page 4

Helen Belopolsky, Miha Hribernik | This Month in Geopolitics: November 2026: Key events, themes and trends to watch in the month ahead |  November 2026
NewZealand’s general election is shaping up as a close and fragmented contest. Both the ruling National
and opposition Labour parties are polling at historically low levels, and smaller parties will likely determine
the next government. In a closely contested vote, investors could see coalition uncertainty and a wider

range of possible outcomes for tax, spending, infrastructure, energy and migration policy.
The National party is campaigning on a platform that would broadly preserve the current emphasis on
fiscal restraint, lower regulation and private-sector investment. A Labour-led government could place
greater weight on public services, housing and climate policy, alongside a proposed capital-gains tax on
investment property. However, because any government will rely on minor parties for survival, the final

policy mix will depend heavily on post-election negotiations.
The meeting will be a test of the EU’s ability to translate the competitiveness agenda into practical
reforms. The focus will be on Europe’s ability to restore competitiveness, mobilize investment and
maintain fiscal discipline. The Eurogroup brings together Euro-area finance ministers and is expected to
consider macroeconomic and fiscal developments, energy market pressures and progress towards the

Savings and Investments Union. ECOFIN brings together all EU finance ministers, and will focus on
financial integration, banking sector competitiveness, and economic coordination. To watch will be
tensions between fiscal consolidation and the need to fund defence, energy security and strategic
investment, alongside progress on integrating Europe’s fragmented capital markets and banking sectors. A
budget-focused Council meeting follows on 10 November.

Implementation of existing commitments, rather than new policy pledges, is expected to be the primary
focus of the next climate summit in Antalya. Building on the shift towards implementation at COP30,
COP31 will focus on translating existing climate commitments into practical action, including through
investment, financing and partnerships. For investors, the key watchpoint will be whether the summit can
deliver tangible commitments that translate existing plans into binding funding pledges for critical

infrastructure resilience, scaled-up renewable energy output, and climate finance.
Key events, issues and themes to watch in November 2026
Listed in chronological order

### Page 5

Helen Belopolsky, Miha Hribernik | This Month in Geopolitics: November 2026: Key events, themes and trends to watch in the month ahead |  November 2026
Melaka’s state election will offer a further gauge of Malaysia’s evolving electoral landscape after
strong recent state-level performances by Barisan Nasional (BN). The contest will also be watched for
its read-through to Prime Minister Anwar Ibrahim’s Pakatan Harapan (PH), following setbacks in other

state elections this year.
BN enters the election from a position of relative strength, while its electoral understanding with PAS
and its allies could provide an early indication of how political alignments are evolving ahead of the next
general election. However, Melaka’s local dynamics mean the result should not be treated as a direct
national forecast.
The anticipated signing of a landmark digital economy agreement is set to be one of the most

significant summit deliverables in years. The 49th ASEAN Summit in Manila will focus on economic
integration, energy security, supply-chain resilience, maritime security, the crisis in Myanmar, and
relations with external partners.
For markets, the formal signing of the ASEAN Digital Economy Framework Agreement (DEFA) is the key
event to watch. Designed as a legally binding treaty, DEFA aims to harmonize regional digital trade rules
and cross-border data flows. Successful implementation is expected to double the projected size of the

region’s digital economy by 2030, boosting it from a projected baseline of US$1 trillion to US$2 trillion
(up from approximately US$300 billion today).
The second EU-UK Summit will be an important test of whether the post-Brexit relationship can move
towards tangible outcomes. Prime Minister Andy Burnham will seek to finalize agreements on food and
agricultural standards, youth mobility, and linking emissions trading systems. Burnham has framed
relations as“not good enough.” To watch will be whether we see concrete deals versus declarations, and

whether leaders are prepared to make the concessions necessary to deepen ties.
Key events, issues and themes to watch in November 2026
Listed in chronological order
49th ASEAN Summit (16-18 November)
Source: istockphoto
EU-UK Summit (20 November)
Melaka state election (14 November)

### Page 6

Helen Belopolsky, Miha Hribernik | This Month in Geopolitics: November 2026: Key events, themes and trends to watch in the month ahead |  November 2026
The upcoming local elections could serve as a political barometer ahead of the 2028 general election–
but only up to a point. Local elections in Taiwan are primarily driven by municipal ‘bread and butter’
economic issues; geopolitics and cross-Strait relations have historically not been at the forefront of

voters’ concerns. While this divergence limits theelections’ predictive value for the January 2028 general
vote, the results can provide a sense of the domestic balance of power.
Elections in Taiwan’s six special municipalities – Taipei, New Taipei, Taichung, Tainan, Taoyuan, and
Kaohsiung – will serve as particularly important watchpoints. Some recent polling suggests a potentially
close contest in Taipei between incumbent mayor Chiang Wan-an (Kuomintang) and legislator Shen Po-

yang (Democratic Progressive Party).
Spain’s snap general election could mark a major turning point for Spanish politics and the EU,
potentially ending eight years of Socialist-led government under Pedro Sánchez and bringing the
conservative People’s Party (PP) to power with support from the far-right Vox. Polling suggests PP is
ahead and a PP-Vox alignment is the only one projected to reach a majority. A rightward shift in one of
the EU’s largest economies could occur just as Europe enters a wider electoral cycle, potentially

complicating consensus on migration, climate policy, Ukraine and European integration.
The Forum comes at a pivotal moment in EU-Latin American relations. It is the EU’s first big commercial
push into Argentina since the EU-Mercosur trade deal began to be provisionally applied on 1 May. The
agreement opens new opportunities for trade, investment and market access. The EU is Argentina’s
largest source of foreign direct investment. Around 800 government, business and financial leaders will

focus on energy, critical minerals, agriculture and infrastructure. The EU’s Global Gateway investment
agenda provides a framework for mobilizing public and private capital. While a key outcome will be the
launch of a structured EU-Argentina investment dialogue, to watch will be the ability of Argentine
officials to allay concerns around policy stability and regulatory predictability.
Key events, issues and themes to watch in November 2026
Listed in chronological order

Source: Nestor Barbitta on Unsplash
EU-Argentina Investment and Business Forum (30 November – 1 December)
Spain general election (29 November)
Taiwan local elections (28 November)

### Page 7

Helen Belopolsky, Miha Hribernik | This Month in Geopolitics: November 2026: Key events, themes and trends to watch in the month ahead |  November 2026
A potential Trump-Kim meeting in November could restart official bilateral dialogue, though North
Korea remains highly unlikely to abandon its nuclear program. South Korea’s National Intelligence
Service said in late September that it saw a roughly 50% chance of US-North Korea talks around the
APEC Summit in Shenzhen, although more recent reporting suggests the prospects may have

diminished. If talks do resume, they would only mark the first step in a long and highly uncertain
diplomatic process.
As we noted earlier this year, North Korea is highly unlikely to agree to denuclearization. Unless renewed
negotiations sidestep denuclearization in favor of a risk-reduction or coexistence framework, dialogue is
likely to stall, not least given Pyongyang’s constitutional pledge to continue expanding its nuclear
arsenal. This would make even a negotiated freeze on further expansion difficult to achieve.

WHAT LESSONS CAN WE LEARN TO KEEP EUROPEAN INDUSTRY COMPETITIVE FROM
THE MEASURES TAKEN BY GERMANY, AS THE COST OF ELECTRICITY CONTINUES TO
TURN THE SCREW?
Marianne Mueller and Pascal Devaux
issue 19
21 October 2026
The 2022 energy crisis clearly highlighted the issue of electricity prices in Europe — and, more broadly, the issue of energy prices. As
a result, European manufacturers’ competitiveness has been severely undermined when compared with the United States and China.

By reigniting tensions over electricity prices, the conflict that began in the Middle East in late February 2026 further underscores the
continent’s vulnerability to fossil fuel imports, despite the expansion of renewable energy. Faced with an environment of potential sharp
and sudden price rises, European governments do not have the same room for manoeuvre.
While the additional costs borne by European manufacturers are mainly linked to the share of gas in national electricity mixes, we will

first illustrate that, in Germany, grid operation and maintenance costs, as well as taxation, are also contributing significantly to these
costs.
We will then explore the solutions proposed by the German government under the new European measures regulating state aid to
electricity-intensive industries.
Finally, our estimates will show that, while these measures make Germany more competitive against some European countries, they
do not place it on an equal competitive footing with the United States or China. Nevertheless, Germany has made significant progress

in decarbonising and making its electricity mix more flexible from a European perspective. This should help to ensure that electricity costs
are reduced in the medium term.

### Page 2

2
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoInsight 19 / 21 October 2026
The bank
for a changing
worldWHAT LESSONS CAN WE LEARN TO KEEP EUROPEAN INDUSTRY COMPETITIVE FROM THE MEA- SURES TAKEN BY GERMANY, AS THE COST OF ELECTRICITY CONTINUES TO TURN THE SCREW?
GAS: A KEY FACTOR IN THE ADDITIONAL ELECTRICITY COSTS
FACED BY EUROPEAN INDUSTRY
European manufacturers are still paying significantly higher elec -
tricity costs than their American and Chinese competitors. In 2019,

there was a 30% difference between the prices charged to European
electricity-intensive industries (corresponding to the lower end of the
industrial price range) and the average US industrial price. In 2025,
it stood at nearly 100% according to the IEA (2026) 1, after peaking at
an average of 148% between 2022 and 2023. There is currently a 50%
difference with their Chinese competitors2.
The disruption to Russian gas supplies since 2022 is behind the rising

gas prices, particularly as the EU has had to turn to liquefied natural
gas3 (LNG), which is more expensive than gas transported via pipe -
line and, above all, exposes Europe to global gas market conditions.
The growth of LNG has, in fact, intensified competition between the
major consumption regions, primarily Europe and Asia. European and
Asian prices now tend to move more in tandem (Chart 1). However, in
China, the prevalence of coal — a widely available local resource —

and a more regulated electricity price help to keep industrial prices
lower. At the same time, the United States enjoys plentiful domestic
gas, which is produced at a low cost, driven by the sharp rise in shale
oil and gas production over the past decade. By contrast, since 2022,
Europe has been more exposed to the cost of imported gas (90% of its
total consumption, according to Eurostat), while the United States and
China have enjoyed plentiful domestic resources.

Fluctuations and the level of gas prices are passed on to Eu -
ropean electricity prices via the ‘merit order’ marginal pricing
mechanism, with the wholesale price determined by the margi -
nal cost of the last (and most expensive) electricity source called
upon to meet demand.
As a result, gas plays a decisive role, with gas-fired power sta -
tions setting the price in around 40% of cases in Europe4, with si-
gnificant variations depending on national electricity mixes. It is

close to this average in Germany, below it in Spain (26%) and
France (17%), and above it in Italy (73%) 5 in 2025 6 7. Therefore,
electricity prices are still sensitive to fluctuations in gas prices,
even when electricity is not generated from gas (Chart 2). This
pass-through is particularly pronounced when demand rises
and requires the use of more expensive sources, or when supply
constraints arise (for example, during extreme heatwaves that
reduce wind-power generation or disrupt the cooling of nuclear

power plants).
1 Prices – Electricity 2026 – Analysis – IEA
2 The difference may be even larger for other manufacturers who do not receive the same support as electricity-intensive sectors.
3 Imports of Russian gas (via pipeline and LNG) accounted for around 45% of total European imports in 2021, compared with 14% in Q2 2026 (Bruegel, natural gas imports).
4 ACER, 2026, Key developments in EU electricity and gas markets, 2026 Monitoring report.
5 In Italy, in response to sharply rising gas prices in 2026, the government is currently negotiating a potential option of capping the prices of gas supplied to power stations in

order to limit the rise in the wholesale price of electricity. However, this can only be a temporary general mechanism, with the potentially major cost being borne by public funds
and/or households.
6 Eurelectric, 2026, Power Barometer.
7 EMBER, 2026, Latest energy shock reminds Europe of its risky reliance on gas.
8 Energy shock: 2026 vs. 2022 dashboard – 2nd October 2026 – Economic Studies – BNP Paribas
However, for the time being, the 2026 energy crisis is having a far

smaller effect on electricity prices than in 2022. On the one hand, the
2026 peak reached by the European TTF benchmark is still well below
that recorded in 20228.
In addition, there has been significant progress in decarbonising Eu -
ropean electricity mixes since 2022, which has helped to mitigate, on
average, the pass-through of gas prices to electricity prices.

SOME EUROPEAN COUNTRIES HAVE MANAGED TO STAY
COMPETITIVE, BUT GERMANY IS STILL THE EXCEPTION
Electricity prices partly vary due to countries’ electricity mixes
(Chart 3). Electricity-intensive manufacturers in countries where the
share of fossil energy is high face higher costs (Poland, Italy and Ire -
land) (Chart 4). Conversely, countries with a less carbon-intensive mix

(particularly France and Spain in recent times) are less directly ex -
posed to these fluctuations, with costs closer to the United States and
China (Chart 5).
The situation in Germany is paradoxical, as, despite progressively
greening its electricity mix and wholesale electricity prices (averaging
90 EUR/MWh over the 2023–2025 period) and average electricity costs
(across all industrial sectors) in line with the European average, the most
energy-intensive industries face higher costs than the European average

(and even more so compared with international competitors). This is
mainly due to grid costs and a tax burden above EU standards (Chart 6).
9 Eurostat data are used to compare prices with the rest of the EU. However, given that electricity prices are not uniform for businesses at a European level, there may be differences
between Eurostat and national sources. Jäger P., 2025, “Cautionary tale or model for Europe? How member states should react to Germany’s new electricity subsidy”, Hertie School,

Jacques Delors Centre.
10 P. Devaux, 2026, “European Union: low carbon transition and energy sovereignty, a path fraught with obstacles”
In the first half of 2026, for the most electricity-intensive industries,
the difference compared with the European average was 9%, based on
the prices excluding taxes and levies provided by Eurostat. This gap is
mainly due to the cost of accessing the grid. If all taxes and levies are
included, the difference rises to 21% (Chart 7)9.

EUROPE’S APPROACH OF ADOPTING BOTH STRUCTURAL
PROGRAMMES AND TARGETED SUPPORT MEASURES
European programmes to support the low-carbon transition pursue
objectives relating to climate action (reducing greenhouse gas emis -
sions), sovereignty10 (increasing energy self-sufficiency) and competi -
tiveness (reducing the volatility and costs associated with fossil fuels).
The RePowerEU programme, which was launched in 2022 in response
to the energy crisis, primarily aims to reduce imports of Russian gas

and, more generally, to accelerate the low-carbon transition. Although
Europe has reduced its dependence on imports of Russian fossil fuels,
there has still not been enough progress around the energy transition
and cross-border connectivity. This is due to governance constraints

and struggles in implementing the programme in practice within each
Member State11.
State aid is still the main vehicle for this. In response to the 2021–
2022 energy crisis, the European Union temporarily relaxed the rules
on this type of aid in order to support businesses facing rising energy
prices. Any intervention of this kind, provided that it is selective and

could affect trade within the single market, must be authorised in ad -
vance by the European Commission in principle. Since June 2025, the
Clean Industrial Deal State Aid Framework (CISAF) has provided a more
sustainable framework for these kinds of interventions. In particular,
this framework, which runs until the end of 2030, authorises Member
States to grant temporary aid to electricity-intensive businesses to off-
set the cost of electricity. The aid must be targeted and come with

decarbonisation commitments12. Against the backdrop of the crisis in
the Middle East, the temporary METSAF scheme, adopted in April 2026,
helped (in relation to measures falling under the CISAF) to increase the
proportion of additional costs offset by governments from 50% to 70%,
thereby offering additional support to the most at-risk sectors.
GERMANY IS STILL SUFFERING FROM INSUFFICIENT IN-
VESTMENT IN ITS ELECTRICITY NETWORKS, WHICH THE
AID SCHEMES ARE ATTEMPTING TO OFFSET

The issue of electricity costs is a particularly hot issue in Germany,
given the importance of industry to the economy (around 20% of va -
lue added, one of the highest levels in Europe) and its exposure to
international competition. Furthermore, the delay in modernising
electricity infrastructure (only 22.6% of the high-voltage lines planned
for 2025 had actually been completed by that date) is causing bottle -
necks between the production areas in the north and the consump -

11 European Court of Auditors, 2026, “Implementation of the REPowerEU plan must be stepped up”.
12 It applies to the most electricity-intensive activities, which are seeing their competitiveness threatened by international competition, and follows the 4x50 principle: 50% of
electricity consumption is eligible for a subsidy; Of the eligible proportion, the subsidy may not exceed 50% of the wholesale price;
The final wholesale price paid by the company must not be less than €50/MWh;

50% of the aid received must be reinvested in decarbonisation (renewable energy generation, storage and improvements in energy efficiency); Around 90 sectors are covered by this
reform, accounting for approximately 2,000 companies, including in the chemical, steel and paper industries. The scheme, which is strictly regulated, will run for just three years
(from 1st January 2026 to the end of 2028) at an estimated total cost of between EUR 3 and 5 bn, financed by the special Climate and Transformation Fund.

13 Bundesnetzagentur Monitoring Report (2015) and Bundesnetzagentur Monitoring Report (2025). These costs mainly cover the costs of physical infrastructure; operational,
regulatory and compliance costs; and costs associated with using the transmission and distribution network.
14 The Industriestrompreis (industrial electricity price) is the public subsidy mechanism aiming to reduce the price of electricity for some industries.
15 The indirect carbon cost compensation mechanism is a government support measure for electricity-intensive industries, which aims to offset the cost of carbon-emission

allowances for energy producers.
tion centres in the south. As a result, the costs of accessing services
have increased fivefold over 10 years (rising from EUR 1 bn in 2014
to EUR 5.2 bn in 2024 13). At the same time, as we have already seen,
taxation and grid charges are hitting industry hard, with levies on large
consumers far exceeding the European average despite some support
mechanisms being put in place for the most electricity-intensive com-

panies (such as the Bandlastprivileg measure to reduce grid tariffs).
As a result, German industrial production has suffered in particular
due to rising energy prices in recent years, including electricity, with
electricity-intensive industries being especially affected (Chart 8).
In order to cope with these higher electricity costs than the European
average, Germany has been using European support schemes to reduce
the cost of electricity for businesses, particularly since the Merz go -

vernment took office in May 2025. The main scheme — the Industries-
trompreis14, made possible by the CISAF — has been approved by the
European Commission for the 2026–2028 period. While it is less bene-
ficial than the indirect carbon cost (ICC) offsetting included in electri -
city prices15, it has a broader scope, meaning that businesses ineligible
for the ICC can benefit from it. Other measures complement this targe-
ted support by addressing other components of the electricity bill. As a

result, the electricity tax has been set at the European minimum for
manufacturing sectors, while the gas-storage tax has been abolished.
The government is also subsidising grid fees, which should halve them.
Overall, according to our estimates based on 2024 prices, the final
price of electricity for these industries could fall by around a quarter
(to 116 EUR/MWh for industries with annual consumption of between
70 and 150 GWh, compared with a European average of 123 EUR/MWh)

(Chart 9). Germany’s advantage over Central and Eastern European
countries, where electricity costs are generally already higher, looks
set to increase.

However, even when taking all of the subsidies into account, the price
of electricity in Germany would remain higher than the levels seen in
France, Spain or Portugal (and even more so in the United States and
China).
FISCAL MEASURES: LIMITED ROOM FOR MANOEUVRE
There are a number of limitations to these European schemes. For
the country implementing them, lower electricity costs mean that

its industry becomes more competitive against industries from other
EU countries, but this does very little to address the disadvantage that
it faces compared with the United States and China. Therefore, this
measure will unlikely be enough to counteract the loss of market share
seen vis-à-vis the United States and China. In addition, the support
schemes are restrictive (conditional on investment in the low-carbon
transition) and are transitional. Finally, their cost is partly passed on

to the public purse or to consumers who do not receive this support
(in Germany, the cost of the new energy price support measures is
estimated at EUR 14.3 bn in 2026, or 0.3% of GDP16).
Some European manufacturers have proposed abolishing or reducing
the carbon tax as a solution to getting electricity prices down quickly.
In Germany, this tax accounted for 14% of the electricity price in the
first half of 2025, which is higher than the EU average (11%). However,

this proposal is already covered by the indirect carbon cost offsetting
mechanism for electricity-intensive industries exposed to carbon al -
lowance prices. In addition, while adjustments are being made — no -
tably to slow the rate at which allowances are reduced — it seems
highly unlikely that the scheme will be significantly challenged. It is
one of the cornerstones of Europe’s policy to combat climate change,
which has been effective thus far, as shown by the falling greenhouse

gas emissions from industry. More broadly, the gradual introduction of
a carbon border adjustment mechanism (CBAM) should mitigate com-
petition distortions caused by environmental practices17.
IN GERMANY AND THE REST OF EUROPE, HEAVY INVEST -
MENT IS DRIVING UP ELECTRICITY BILLS IN THE SHORT
TERM
In response to the challenge of the low-carbon transition and indus -
trial competitiveness, European governments must strike a balance
between decarbonising the electricity mix and ensuring a supply of

energy that is flexible enough (particularly from controllable sources)
to guarantee stable electricity systems. The integration of an increa -
sing proportion of intermittent renewable energy sources requires
massive investment in transmission and storage infrastructure (statio-
nary batteries). This expenditure, which places a long-term burden on
grid costs, reduces the scope for support schemes (particularly public
ones) to address competitiveness gaps. In Germany, the cost of the

transition is estimated at between EUR 122 bn, according to the go -
vernment (2.8% of GDP), and EUR 200 bn, according to the DIHK18 (4.6%
of GDP), by 2030–2035. If we take the ECB’s assumption19 that govern-
ments will cover around 17% of these investments, the annual public
16 The Industriestrompreis is estimated at around EUR 1.3 bn per year, the subsidy for network charges is projected to amount to EUR 6.5 bn in 2026, the abolition of the gas sur -

charge around EUR 3.4 bn, and the reduction in the electricity tax nearly EUR 3 bn per year. Furthermore, the ICC had cost EUR 2.3 bn in 2024 for costs incurred in 2023.

expenditure burden of the transition in Germany would be between
0.5 and 0.8 percentage points of GDP by 2030. However, this budgetary
pressure comes at the same time as three other budgetary increases:
rising social expenditure linked to an ageing population; the necessary
increase to defence budgets to meet NATO’s target of 3.5% of GDP by
2029; and the rising cost of debt-servicing20.
Germany has already made significant progress in maximising its use

of renewable energy, putting it at the head of the pack in Europe. The
country generates 20% of Europe’s renewable electricity (solar and
wind) and accounts for 30% of installed storage capacity (BESS) 21, far
ahead of other European countries (ranking third globally). However,
despite this momentum, Germany’s dependence on gas has not yet
decreased. As a matter of fact, it is only partially offsetting the planned
closure of coal-fired power stations and the phasing out of nuclear

reactors. Furthermore, the intermittent nature of solar and wind power
could prolong this dependence in the short and medium term. In order
to ensure that the German electricity system is flexible, new invest -
ments in gas-fired power plants, supported by public subsidies, are
planned22.

SUBSIDIES ARE HELPING TO ADDRESS THE EMERGENCY
CAUSED BY ENERGY CRISES WITHOUT RESTORING THE
INTERNATIONAL COMPETITIVENESS OF ENERGY-INTEN-
SIVE INDUSTRIES
In response to the impact of energy crises on industrial competitive -
ness, European governments are finding themselves having to strike a
delicate balance between providing short-term support to industries

exposed to increased international competition and continuing — if
not, accelerating — energy transition policies which will result in hi -
gher costs for electricity-network users for several years to come.
Beyond direct economic support measures, which are difficult to sus -
tain long term, four priority areas are emerging:
1/ Demonstrating flexibility in implementing European directives in
order to factor in national specificities and the differing timeframes

between energy crises (which can lead to irreversible industrial conse-
quences) and reforms (which have a longer-term horizon). The relaxa-
tion of the European carbon tax mechanism 23 appears to be a step
towards this.
2/ Continuing efforts to decarbonise the electricity mix, which are still
the main vehicle for sustainably reducing electricity prices for Euro -
pean industries in the medium and long term.
3/ Optimising the use of electricity networks on the demand side, in

particular through smart grids, a flagship element of the Commission’s
COM (2026) 600 reform presented last July24.
4/ Coordinating European electricity networks in order to harness the
complementarities between national systems.
Article completed on 19 October 2026

《盤勢看法》
科技類股於10月中旬出現明顯修正，不過市場資金
並未明顯撤離，而是在其他類股輪動，使標普500
指數的修正幅度相對有限。長期來說，穩健的經濟
基本面才是股市行情延續的關鍵，而目前的景氣和
企業獲利支持這樣的趨勢，只是高利率環境仍將使
市場資金更加嚴格看待資產的成長性。拉美方面，
現任左翼總統魯拉在10月初第一輪投票落後的情況
下，於第二輪決選中逆轉，市場重新定價財政前景，
利率上升壓力將提高。據此，將拉美和巴西股市的
評等由正向下調為中立。

《貨幣政策-聯準會偏鷹發言但不急於連續升息》
10月FOMC會議如市場預期維持政策利率在3.75%-
4.0%不變，本次會議的重點可歸納如下：
◼ 聯準會認為美國經濟非常強勁，消費支出健康，
企業投資強勁，通膨仍處於偏高水準，「但也
不急於立刻升息」。華許的解釋並不是通膨風
險已經消失，而是認為先前的政策行動已經透
過金融條件產生緊縮效果，投資人開始更加審
慎地配置資金，部分資產的信用利差擴大。
◼ 縮減資產負債表不是當前政策的優先選項，相
關問題仍交由工作小組研究，這表示雖然他在
長期制度設計上支持重新檢討聯準會資產負債
表的規模與功能，也不代表短期內就會立即推
動更激進的量化緊縮。
雖然華許一如既往地不提供對未來利率路徑的承諾，

但從記者會的發言可大致推論，目前的方案是間隔
一次會議再採取下一個新的行動，本次會議暫停升
息，12月會議將進行下一次升息。2027年1月的會
議按兵不動，3月會議視市場情況再決定。本行的基
準情境是，2027年開始會看到關稅效應、能源衝擊
對通膨的影響逐漸消退，因此不認為2027年3月有
升息的必要性。風險則在於美伊衝突進一步升級，
使企業利用年初例行漲價的機會將成本壓力轉嫁給
消費者，若此，3月可能再升息一次。但本行仍定調
9月以來的升息決策為「風險管理式的升息」，而非
持續性的升息循環。加上華許並未將縮減資產負債
表列入優先事項，過度緊縮的風險不大。

• 本行仍定調9月以來的升息決
